Zero-init LoRA starts at a singular point where the \(A\)-direction is dead at first order, so LoRA and LoRA-FA, trained with the same optimizer from the same \(A_0\), agree to first order (Lemma II.4). Split initialisations start at a smooth point with full first-order reach. That this explains the faster early progress reported for PiSSA and LoRA-GA is a conjecture; LoRA-GA credits gradient alignment, and MiLoRA also starts at a smooth point.

CreditHayou et al., 2024 compare the two zero-factor starts, “Init[A]” and “Init[B]”, through their training dynamics; here \(S_1\) already tells them apart. The facts about \(\mathcal M_{\le r}\) used in the proof are classical.
More: the first-order deficit and the data

\(S_1\) counts directions in weight space. The lazy-regime kernel \(J_f\,d\rho\,\Lambda\,d\rho^\top J_f^\top\) also involves the network Jacobian \(J_f\) and the optimizer’s metric \(\Lambda\), and for random-\(A_0\) zero-init LoRA it approximates the full fine-tuning kernel (Malladi et al., 2023). A large first-order deficit need not cost much on the data.

Counting first-order directions

A first step of LoRA from \((B,A)\) adds \(s(\dot BA+B\dot A)\), a matrix with rows in \(\mathrm{row}\,A\) plus one with columns in \(\mathrm{col}\,B\). Counting both families and their overlap gives \(\dim S_1\).

Proposition II.3 (first-order rank formula: smoothness, alignment and scale). For \(\kappa(B,A)=W_{\rm base}+sBA\) with \(s\ne0\),
\[\operatorname{rank}d\kappa_{(B,A)}=m\operatorname{rk}A+n\operatorname{rk}B-\operatorname{rk}A\operatorname{rk}B\qquad\text{for all }(B,A),\]

and \(\{XA\}\cap\{BY\}=\{BRA:R\in\mathbb R^{r\times r}\}\). For full-rank frames this overlap is the tangent space of the product \(\mathrm{FA}(A)\times\mathrm{FB}(B)\) of Prop I.8(a), re-based at \(\kappa(B,A)\), whose image is the meet of the two one-sided images. It is a generalised LoRA-XS, and LoRA-XS proper when \((B,A)\) span the top-\(r\) singular subspaces of \(W_0\) (with \(\sigma_r\gt0\)), as at a PiSSA init.

Under gradient flow \(\dot B=-\eta_B\nabla_BL\), \(\dot A=-\eta_A\nabla_AL\), with \(G=\nabla_WL\),

\[\tfrac{d}{dt}L=-s^2\big(\eta_B\|GA^\top\|_F^2+\eta_A\|B^\top G\|_F^2\big)\]

at every time, in particular at \((B_0,A_0)\). To first order in the step sizes the same holds for full-batch gradient descent, and in expectation for unbiased minibatch SGD. Under Adam (\(\varepsilon\to0\)) the first step instead lowers the loss, to first order in the step sizes, by \(s\big(\eta_B\|GA_0^\top\|_1+\eta_A\|B_0^\top G\|_1\big)\) for \(s\gt0\) (by the same expression with \(\lvert s\rvert\) in general): entrywise \(\ell_1\) norms, linear in \(s\).

(expand)

The image of \(d\kappa\) is \(\{XA\}+\{BY\}\), since the factor \(s\ne0\) does not change it. Its summands are the matrices with row space in \(\mathrm{row}\,A\), of dimension \(m\operatorname{rk}A\), and the matrices with column space in \(\mathrm{col}\,B\), of dimension \(n\operatorname{rk}B\). A matrix \(D\) lies in both iff \(D\in\mathrm{col}\,B\otimes\mathrm{row}\,A\), of dimension \(\operatorname{rk}A\operatorname{rk}B\), and then \(D=B(B^+DA^+)A\).

The gradient-flow rate is \(\langle G,\dot W\rangle\) with \(\dot W=-s^2(\eta_BGA^\top A+\eta_ABB^\top G)\), because \(\nabla_BL=sGA^\top\) and \(\nabla_AL=sB^\top G\). Adam’s first step is \(\delta B=-\eta_B\,\mathrm{sign}(sGA^\top)\), so \(\langle G,s\,\delta B\,A\rangle=-\lvert s\rvert\eta_B\|GA^\top\|_1\), and likewise for \(A\).

Numerical check: the rank formula holds in 400 random cases, including rank-deficient factors, and the rate matches finite differences.∎

Example (one attention projection of a 7B model). At \(4096\times4096\) with \(r=16\), zero-factor initialisations (LoRA, QLoRA, EVA, LoRA-FA) have \(mr=65{,}536\) first-order directions and split ones \(r(m+n-r)=130{,}816\), the dimension of LoRA’s image.

“Better initialisation” mixes three mechanisms, and the proposition separates them.

  • Smoothness. PiSSA and LoRA-GA start off the apex, so their first step sees all \(r(m+n-r)\) directions.
  • Alignment. At a zero-\(B\) start the rate is \(s^2\eta_B\|GA_0^\top\|_F^2\). Among \(A_0\) with orthonormal rows, the top-\(r\) right singular space of \(G\) maximises it, and that is LoRA-GA’s \(A_0\). Its \(B_0\), the next \(r\) left singular vectors, makes the first step approximate full fine-tuning and does not maximise the rate. EVA’s activation frame is a gradient-free proxy. Under Adam the rate depends on the basis of \(A_0\), so this alignment belongs to gradient flow and SGD.
  • Scale. \(\|GA_0^\top\|_F^2\) grows with \(\|A_0\|^2\), and initial scale trades off against learning rate (Thm III.10). HF’s Kaiming default has \(A_0A_0^\top\approx I/3\) and EVA with whiten=False has \(A_0A_0^\top=I\), so fair comparisons normalise \(A_0A_0^\top\).
More: EVA’s frame and the factor scales

EVA’s guarantee assumes inputs independent of the backpropagated errors and bounds the expected per-sample squared gradient norm, a different quantity from the rate above, and HF’s EVA centres the activations. PiSSA’s \(S_r^{1/2}\)-weighted factors and LoRA-GA’s stability scaling also change factor norms (both factors in LoRA-GA’s paper, only \(B\) in HF’s default).

CreditThe gradient-aligned frame is due to Wang et al., 2024 (LoRA-GA), the activation frame to Paischer et al., 2024 (EVA), and the spectral split to Meng et al., 2024 (PiSSA). The proposition only sorts their effects into three terms.

The zero gate

At a zero-\(B\) start, \(BA\) is linear in \(B\) and \(B_0=0\), so moving \(A\) alone changes nothing. The same holds whenever a trainable gate starts closed. Below, \(K=D\rho\,\Lambda\,D\rho^\top\) is the cometric on weights induced by an optimizer’s metric \(\Lambda\) (Exposé III).

Lemma II.4 (zero gate). Let \(Q=U\times V\) with \(U\) a vector space, let \(\rho=\theta_0+\beta(u,v)\) with \(\beta\) smooth, and suppose \(\beta(u_0,v)=0\) for all \(v\); for example \(\beta(\cdot,v)\) linear and \(u_0=0\). Then \(D_v\rho_{q_0}=0\). Hence \(\nabla_v(L\circ\rho)(q_0)=0\), \(S_1=D_u\beta(u_0,v_0)(U)\) (\(=\beta(U,v_0)\) in the linear case), and the cometric at \(q_0\) is \(K_{q_0}=D_u\beta\,\Lambda_{uu}\,D_u\beta^\top\), where \(\Lambda_{uu}\) is the \(uu\)-block of \(\Lambda\); no block-diagonality is needed, since the \(v\)-columns of \(D\rho\) vanish. The weight trajectory \(\theta(t)\) agrees with that of the method with \(v\) frozen at \(v_0\) to first order in \(t\) (gradient flow), or in the step size (one or more Adam steps). The parameter \(v\) itself may still move: under terms not of the form \(L\circ\rho\) (regularisers, decoupled weight decay), and under Adam, whose step-2 move of \(v\) is \(O(\eta)\) although its effect on \(\theta\) is \(O(\eta^2)\).

Function-level variant. For an extension with realised function \(F(u,v,x)\), if \(F(u_0,v,x)=F_0(x)\) for all \(v\) and \(x\), then \(D_vF=0\) at \(q_0\), with the same conclusions at the level of functions. No linearity is needed.

(expand)

Since \(\beta(u_0,v)=0\) for all \(v\), differentiating in \(v\) gives \(D_v\beta(u_0,v_0)=0\). The rest is the chain rule. The gradient \(\nabla_v(L\circ\rho)=D_v\rho^\top\nabla L\) vanishes, \(S_1\) is the image of the \(u\)-columns alone, and in \(K=D\rho\,\Lambda\,D\rho^\top\) every term that touches a \(v\)-column is zero, which leaves \(D_u\beta\,\Lambda_{uu}\,D_u\beta^\top\). The function-level variant is the same argument applied to \(F\).

Numerical check: under Adam with \(\eta\in\{10^{-2},10^{-3},10^{-4}\}\), the step-2 move of \(A\) in zero-init LoRA scales as \(\eta\), while the deviation of \(\Delta W\) from the frozen-\(A\) method scales as \(\eta^2\).∎

Instances, assuming no weight decay on \(v\):

  • LoRA (\(u=B\)) and VeRA (\(u=b\)).
  • AdaLoRA, with \(u\) its diagonal of singular values started at \(0\). \(\theta\) first moves only along the \(r\) directions \(p_iq_i^\top\), so \(\dim S_1=r\) for generic \(P,Q\). \(P\) and \(Q\) move at step one through the orthogonality penalty (HF default orth_reg_weight=0.5), with no first-order effect on \(\theta\).
  • LoHa with a zero factor (HF zero-initialises hada_w2_b).
  • By the function-level variant, the gate of LLaMA-Adapter and bottleneck adapters whose up-projection starts at zero (Houlsby et al., 2019 start near that point).

HRA’s paired initialisation, though of a different form, shows the same first-order collapse (Thm II.7(b)).

CreditZhu et al., 2024 find over whole runs that a random, untrained \(A\) does nearly as well as a tuned one, and that training \(B\) alone gives better generalisation bounds. Lemma II.4 is only the first-order statement.

Off the point: quantised bases

QLoRA quantises the frozen weight, so it is LoRA pointed at \(\kappa\theta_0\), with pointing defect \(e=\kappa\theta_0-\theta_0\) (Def V.1, Prop V.2(a)). As \(e\) is generically of full rank, \(\theta_0\) lies outside \(\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{LoRA}_r(\kappa\theta_0)\) unless \(\operatorname{rank}e\le r\), which is why Theorem II.1 speaks of exact pointings. When the initialisation also splits, the order of operations decides the defect (Prop V.2(b)). Below, \(W\) is the full-precision weight, \(e=\kappa W-W\) and \(\delta_0=\delta(q_0)\).

pipelineexamplesdefect \(\rho(q_0)-W\)
quantise, then zero-initQLoRA\(e\)
split, then quantise the residualQPiSSA (Meng et al., 2024); HF’s recommended PiSSA/CorDA workflow; LoftQ’s later quantisation steps\(\kappa(W_{\rm res})-W_{\rm res}\)
quantise, then fit the errorLoftQ with one iteration (HF default loftq_iter=1); HF replace_lora_weights_loftq\(e-\mathrm{SVD}_r(e)\), the part of \(e\) left after its best rank-\(r\) approximation (unit LoRA scale)
quantise, split, re-quantiseHF olora_init on bitsandbytes weights\(e+\kappa(r')-r'\), with \(r'=\kappa W-\delta_0\)
CaveatHF PEFT’s LoftQ initialiser does not divide by the LoRA scale \(s=\alpha/r\), so for \(s\ne1\) the deployed defect is \(e-s\,\mathrm{SVD}_r(e)\). At \(s=2\) its Frobenius norm equals \(\|e\|\), and the fit buys nothing over QLoRA in that norm.
CreditQuantised bases for LoRA come from Dettmers et al., 2023 (QLoRA), fitting the adapter to the quantisation error from Li et al., 2023 (LoftQ), and the quantised PiSSA variant from Meng et al., 2024.

The strata of Theorem II.2 classify what a start can express. The dynamics see a finer quotient. For a zero-\(B\) start, SGD sees \(A_0^\top A_0\) and Adam sees \(A_0\) up to signed permutations of its rows (Cor III.13). A dynamical classification of initialisations, including the degenerate pointings, is open.

Exposé II.B

Erlangen: what a method can never change

A method that moves the pretrained weight by a group element can never change that group's invariants. Orbits also place HRA as a meet, DoRA as a saturation and the scaling methods on a ladder.

A geometry is what a group leaves unchanged. Before asking how a method learns, ask what it cannot help keeping.

after Klein
In one minute
  • A method whose weights are always \(W_0R\), with \(R\) exactly orthogonal, can never change \(WW^\top\), so neuron norms, angles and singular values survive any number of merges in exact arithmetic. HF PEFT's default OFT, BOFT's scale and GOFT's default scaler break this.
  • For injective \(W_0\) and even \(r\le n-2\), HRA reaches exactly the rank-\(r\) LoRA updates of the form \(W_0R\), \(R\in SO(n)\). Its HF PEFT default initialisation is an apex.
  • DoRA reaches exactly the weights of LoRA plus a free gain per output neuron. From the same start, any gap between the two comes from training.
  • (IA)³, SSF's scales and DoRA's magnitude lie in the image of rank-one HiRA, \(W_0\odot(J+BA)\), which for \(W_0\) without zeros is LoRA seen through \(X\mapsto W_0\odot X\).

Klein's Erlangen programme of 1872 defines a geometry by its group of motions and studies what the motions leave unchanged. OFT, GOFT, HRA and ETHER multiply the pretrained matrix by an orthogonal matrix, (IA)³ and SSF's scale multiply it by a diagonal one, and DoRA's magnitude does the same to a LoRA weight. These are methods of action type (Definition I.2), \(\rho(q)=\gamma(q)\cdot W_0\) with \(\gamma\) landing in a group. Whatever that group cannot change, the method cannot change, in training or after any number of merges.

CreditKlein's programme is the model. Bronstein et al., 2021 made it the organising principle of geometric deep learning, for architectures. Here it is applied to the fine-tuning map.
Analogy (what “Erlangen” means here). For a group-type method the Erlangen reading is a theorem, since its image lies in one orbit. LoRA's cone \(W_0+\mathcal M_{\le r}\) has a singular vertex at \(W_0\) (Theorem II.2(c)), so it is no orbit; for cones the word names the shape of the image and its covariance group (Definition II.14).

One convention matters. A linear box computes \(y=Wx\) with \(W\in\mathbb R^{m\times n}\), and row \(i\) of \(W\) is output neuron \(i\). The two Gram matrices, over neurons and over inputs, are

\[K_{\rm out}(W)=WW^\top,\qquad K_{\rm in}(W)=W^\top W.\]

A right multiplication \(W\mapsto WR\) acts on the input side and fixes \(K_{\rm out}\); a left multiplication \(W\mapsto PW\) acts on the output side and fixes \(K_{\rm in}\). Papers that store \(W^\top\) swap these words, and we translate every method into this convention.

Complete invariants

A complete invariant of a group action agrees on two matrices exactly when one can be carried to the other. For the five actions in PEFT it is classical. We write \(T_m\) for the invertible diagonal matrices, \(T_m^+=\{\mathrm{diag}(d):d_i\gt0\}\) for its identity component, and \(\odot\) for the entrywise product.

Theorem II.5 (complete invariants of the basic actions). Let \(W,W'\in\mathbb R^{m\times n}\).
  1. Right orthogonal (input side). \(W'=WR\) for some \(R\in O(n)\) iff \(WW^\top=W'W'^\top\). For \(SO(n)\), when \(\operatorname{rank}W=n\), also require that the then unique \(R\) have \(\det R=1\).
  2. Left orthogonal. \(W'=PW\) for some \(P\in O(m)\) iff \(W^\top W=W'^\top W'\).
  3. Two-sided. \(W'=PWR\) for some \(P\in O(m)\) and \(R\in O(n)\) iff \(W\) and \(W'\) have the same singular values, counted with multiplicity.
  4. Left torus. If \(W\) has no zero row, \(W'=DW\) for some \(D\in T_m^+\) iff \(w_i'\in\mathbb R_{\gt0}\,w_i\) for every \(i\). For the full torus \(T_m\), whose signs are unconstrained, the complete invariant is the set of zero rows together with the row lines.
  5. Full Hadamard torus. \(W'=E\odot W\) for some \(E\in(\mathbb R^\times)^{m\times n}\) iff \(W\) and \(W'\) have the same zero pattern.
(expand)

(1) If all inner products of rows agree, the map \(\sum_ic_iw_i\mapsto\sum_ic_iw_i'\) is well defined and isometric on the row spans, because \(\|\sum_ic_iw_i\|^2=c^\top WW^\top c\). The two row spans have the same dimension, \(\operatorname{rank}WW^\top\). Extend the map by an isometry of the orthogonal complements to \(R^\top\in O(n)\); then \(W'=WR\). The converse is immediate.

(2) is (1) applied to transposes. (3) follows from the singular value decomposition. (4) and (5) hold entry by entry; for (5) put \(E_{ij}=W'_{ij}/W_{ij}\) on the support and \(1\) off it. ∎

More: where Theorem II.5 comes from

Item (1) follows from the first fundamental theorem of invariant theory for \(O(n)\), or from Witt's extension of isometries. LoHa's gauge in Proposition II.12, the two-sided diagonal torus \(\{\mathrm{diag}(a)\,X\,\mathrm{diag}(b)\}\), differs from the full Hadamard torus in (5).

No PEFT method realises (4) or (5) as a group action. (IA)³ gains and DoRA magnitudes are unconstrained reals, and HiRA's factor \(J+BA\) can vanish entrywise. These methods can create new zeros, so only the one-way statement transfers, that the zeros of \(W_0\) remain zero.

actiongroupcomplete invariantmethods that live here
\(W\mapsto WR\), input side\(O(n)\)\(K_{\rm out}=WW^\top\)exact-Cayley OFT and block OFT, HRA, and GOFT and ETHER as published
\(W\mapsto PW\), output side\(O(m)\)\(K_{\rm in}=W^\top W\)LyCORIS diag-OFT; HF PEFT OFT up to version 0.13
\(W\mapsto PWR\)\(O(m)\times O(n)\)singular values with multiplicityany composite of the two rows above
\(W\mapsto DW\)\(T_m^+\), \(T_m\)row rays; zero rows and row lines(IA)³ on \(k,v\), SSF's scale, DoRA's magnitude (closure only)
\(W\mapsto E\odot W\)\((\mathbb R^\times)^{m\times n}\)zero patternHiRA (one way only: \(Z(W_0)\subseteq Z(W)\))

Moving a method from the input side to the output side trades \(K_{\rm out}\) for \(K_{\rm in}\). Letting a scale change sign trades rays for lines, and letting it reach zero leaves only the zeros of \(W_0\).

What exactly orthogonal methods can never do

If every weight a method reaches is \(W_0R\) with \(R\) orthogonal, Theorem II.5(1) and (3) freeze the neuron Gram matrix and the singular values, and merging cannot help, since products of orthogonal matrices are orthogonal. The work lies in saying which implementations are exactly orthogonal, and on which side.

Corollary II.6 (what exactly orthogonal methods can never do). Let a method have image in \(W_0\cdot O(n)\) (input side). Examples are OFT and block OFT with an exactly orthogonal parametrisation (the paper's exact Cayley map; HF PEFT with use_cayley_neumann=False, on linear and convolutional layers), GOFT as in its paper (official code with no_scaling=True), HRA, and ETHER as defined in its paper (not ETHER+). Then, in exact arithmetic and through any number of merge-and-restart cycles:
  • it preserves \(K_{\rm out}\), hence every neuron norm, every angle between nonzero neurons, and the “hyperspherical energy”, a function of these angles;
  • it preserves the whole singular spectrum of \(W_0\);
  • block-diagonal OFT with input blocks \(J\) and the same partition in every cycle preserves each partial Gram matrix \(W_{:,J}W_{:,J}^\top\).

Several methods that look orthogonal fail the hypothesis. Each row below is a separate computation.

implementationmapexactly orthogonal?what it keeps
BOFT in HF PEFT; HF PEFT OFT 0.14–0.15\(\mathrm{diag}(s)\,W_0R^\top\), \(R\) exactly orthogonal, \(s\) free, initialised at \(1\)only \(R\)\(K_{\rm out}\mapsto\mathrm{diag}(s)K_{\rm out}\mathrm{diag}(s)\), and block Grams likewise. \(\lvert\cos_{ij}\rvert\) survives when \(s_is_j\ne0\), \(\cos_{ij}\) when \(s_is_j\gt0\). At HF's default single butterfly factor, BOFT is block OFT times \(s\).
GOFT, official code default\(W_0\,\mathrm{diag}(g)\,G\), with a trainable input scale \(g\)noNothing on this list, since \(K_{\rm out}=W_0\mathrm{diag}(g)^2W_0^\top\).
output-side OFT (LyCORIS diag-OFT at its defaults; HF PEFT OFT up to 0.13)image in \(O(m)\cdot W_0\)yes, on the other side\(K_{\rm in}\), the partial input Grams and the spectrum. Not \(K_{\rm out}\).
HF PEFT default OFT since 0.18 (use_cayley_neumann=True)\(R=I+2Q+2Q^2\) \(+\,2Q^3+Q^4\) \(=C(Q)(I-Q^4)\), with \(C\) an exact Cayley mapno, \(R^\top R=(I-Q^4)^2\)Nothing exactly. The adapted weight already has \(K_{\rm out}=W_0(I-Q^4)^2W_0^\top\), and for small \(Q\) each merge shrinks \(K_{\rm out}\) and the spectrum further. (In 0.16–0.17, \(R^\top R=I+4Q^6+4Q^8\).)
qGOFTfree \(2\times2\) blocks with a soft orthogonality penaltynoNeither \(K_{\rm out}\) nor the cosines. Once \(n\gt2\), one non-orthogonal block, conformal or not, generically moves a cosine.
ETHER+two-sided, \(I-uu^\top+vv^\top\), singular when \(u\perp v\)noNeither \(K_{\rm out}\) nor the cosines.
(expand)

Use Theorem II.5(1) and (3), and the fact that a product of orthogonal factors is orthogonal, so the image of any number of merge cycles stays in \(W_0\cdot O(n)\). For block-diagonal \(R\), \((WR)_{:,J}=W_{:,J}R_J\), which gives the partial Grams when the partition is fixed.

For BOFT, \(K_{\rm out}(\mathrm{diag}(s)W_0R^\top)=\mathrm{diag}(s)K_{\rm out}(W_0)\mathrm{diag}(s)\), so \(\cos'_{ij}=\mathrm{sign}(s_is_j)\cos_{ij}\) whenever \(s_is_j\ne0\). All cosines therefore survive when the \(s_i\) are nonzero and share one sign, and when no cosine of \(W_0\) vanishes this is also necessary. For GOFT's default code, \(K_{\rm out}(W_0\mathrm{diag}(g)G)=W_0\mathrm{diag}(g)GG^\top\mathrm{diag}(g)W_0^\top=W_0\mathrm{diag}(g)^2W_0^\top\).

For the Cayley–Neumann map, \((I-Q)(I+Q+Q^2+Q^3)=I-Q^4\), so \(R=(I+Q)(I+Q+Q^2+Q^3)=C(Q)(I-Q^4)\) with \(C(Q)=(I+Q)(I-Q)^{-1}\), which is HF's exact map \((I-Q)(I+Q)^{-1}\) evaluated at \(-Q\). \(C(Q)\) is orthogonal and commutes with \(Q^4\), and \((Q^4)^\top=Q^4\) for skew \(Q\). Hence \(R^\top R=(I-Q^4)^2\), and if the eigenvalues of \(Q\) are \(\pm i\theta_k\), the singular values of \(R\) are \(\lvert1-\theta_k^4\rvert\): at most \(1\) when every \(\theta_k\le2^{1/4}\), and \(0\) when some \(\theta_k=1\). With last coefficient \(2\), as in PEFT 0.16–0.17, the same computation gives \(R^\top R=I+4Q^6+4Q^8\). ∎

Numerical check: with \(s=(-0.5,1,\dots,1)\), BOFT changes a cosine by \(1.5\) while \(\lvert\cos\rvert\) is fixed to \(4\times10^{-16}\), and with every \(s_i\lt0\) the cosines are fixed to \(4\times10^{-16}\). HF's default OFT satisfies \(R^\top R=(I-Q^4)^2\) to \(5\times10^{-16}\), with \(\lVert R^\top R-I\rVert=1.5\times10^{-2}\) already for skew entries of size about \(0.07\) at block size \(8\). A conformal block \(\sqrt2\,\mathrm{Rot}(0.3)\) on two of \(n=8\) inputs changes some cosine by \(0.17\).

CreditQiu et al., 2023 introduced orthogonal fine-tuning, motivated by preserving hyperspherical energy (Liu et al., 2018); their re-scaled OFT (Sec. 3.5) adds the positive per-neuron scale that HF BOFT carries. BOFT is due to Liu et al., 2023, building on butterfly factorisations (Dao et al., 2019); GOFT and qGOFT to Ma et al., 2024 (official code); ETHER and ETHER+ to Bini et al., 2024; HRA to Yuan et al., 2024. The Cayley–Neumann default, BOFT's scale and the output-side merge are read from the Hugging Face PEFT source (OFT config, BOFT layer).

“Orthogonal fine-tuning preserves knowledge” corresponds to preserving a complete invariant, which exact orthogonality keeps through any number of merges (Theorem V.3, corollary 7), up to rounding. HF's default OFT is only close to orthogonal; with block size \(8\) and fresh skew generators with entries near \(0.05\) in every cycle, its merges shrink \(K_{\rm out}\) by about \(0.2\%\), \(1.3\%\), \(6\%\) and \(22\%\) after 1, 10, 50 and 200 cycles (relative Frobenius change, averaged over draws). The statement concerns reach. It does not show that the invariant causes retention, and the control study of ETHER (Bini et al., 2024) found a non-orthogonal multiplicative variant comparably stable.

HRA is a meet of two images

A Householder reflection \(H_u=I-2uu^\top/\|u\|^2\) is orthogonal and differs from \(I\) by rank one. So \(W_0H_{u_1}\cdots H_{u_r}\) lies in the orthogonal orbit of \(W_0\) and in LoRA's cone \(W_0+\mathcal M_{\le r}\), and for injective \(W_0\) and even \(r\) the Cartan–Dieudonné theorem says that HRA fills the cone's intersection with the rotation orbit. HF PEFT repeats each reflection at initialisation, so to first order each pair can only turn its own vector against the rest of the space, and HRA starts at an apex, as zero-init LoRA does (Theorem II.2).

Theorem II.7 (HRA is the meet of LoRA with the rotation orbit, and it starts at an apex). Let \(W_0\in\mathbb R^{m\times n}\) be injective (\(\operatorname{rank}W_0=n\)), and let \(r\) be even with \(2\le r\le n-2\). Define \(\mathrm{HRA}_r:(u_1,\dots,u_r)\mapsto W_0H_{u_1}\cdots H_{u_r}\) with \(u_i\ne0\), where \(H_u=I-2uu^\top/\|u\|^2\). Write \(\mathrm{OFT}_{SO}:=W_0\cdot SO(n)\) for the rotation orbit.
  1. Meet. \(\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{HRA}_r=(W_0+\mathcal M_{\le r})\cap W_0\cdot SO(n)\); the \(O(n)\)-orbit would add odd products such as \(W_0H_u\). Further, \(d(\mathrm{HRA}_r)=rn-\tfrac{r(r+1)}2\), so the generic fibre has dimension \(\tfrac{r(r+1)}2=rn-d\), and this gauge is non-linear.
  2. First-order collapse. At the paired initialisation \(u_{2i-1}=u_{2i}=w_i\) (\(i\le k=r/2\), with the \(w_i\) independent and \(U=\mathrm{span}\{w_i\}\)), \[S_1=W_0\cdot\{\Omega\in\mathfrak{so}(n):P_{U^\perp}\Omega P_{U^\perp}=0\},\qquad\dim S_1=kn-\tfrac{k(k+1)}2\lt d(\mathrm{HRA}_r).\]
  3. Vertex. The germ of \(\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{HRA}_r\) at \(W_0\) is diffeomorphic to the germ at \(0\) of the skew-determinantal cone \(\Sigma_r=\{\Omega\in\mathfrak{so}(n):\operatorname{rank}\Omega\le r\}\), which is not linear. So \(W_0\) is a singular point (the vertex) of the image. Together with (b), the paired initialisation is an apex.
  4. Scope.
    • Not a product. (a) is a meet in the poset of subsets of \(\Theta\) containing \(W_0\) (Observation I.4(c)), not a categorical product of \(\mathrm{LoRA}_r\) and \(\mathrm{OFT}_{SO}\). Over a generic point of the meet, the fibre product \(\{(B,A,R):W_0+BA=W_0R\}\) has local dimension \(d+r^2=rn+\tfrac{r(r-1)}2\ne rn\).
    • Exactly orthogonal OFT is smaller. Exact Cayley maps never produce a rotation with eigenvalue \(-1\). For exact full-block Cayley OFT, \((W_0+\mathcal M_{\le r})\cap\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{OFT}\) is dense in \(\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{HRA}_r\) but misses \(W_0H_{e_1}H_{e_2}\), and for exact block OFT with \(b\lt n\) it is a proper subset. HF PEFT's default Cayley–Neumann OFT is not orthogonal (Corollary II.6); its meet with \(W_0+\mathcal M_{\le r}\) contains non-orthogonal points and is incomparable with \(\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{HRA}_r\).
    • Implementation. The theorem concerns HF's default HRA, without Gram–Schmidt (apply_GS=False); the paired initialisation is HF's default and is not stated in the HRA paper. HF HRA acts on in_features, so injectivity requires out_features \(\ge\) in_features, which excludes down_proj and GQA key/value projections.
(expand)

(a) “\(\subseteq\)”. Each \(H_u-I\) has rank one, so \(R=\prod_iH_{u_i}\) fixes \(\bigcap_iu_i^\perp\) and \(\operatorname{rank}(R-I)\le r\). Also \(\det R=(-1)^r=1\). Hence \(W_0R-W_0=W_0(R-I)\) has rank at most \(r\).

“\(\supseteq\)”. Suppose \(W_0R=W_0+X\) with \(\operatorname{rank}X\le r\) and \(R\in SO(n)\). Injectivity gives \(k':=\operatorname{rank}(R-I)\le r\). By the Cartan–Dieudonné theorem in Scherk's sharp form, \(R\) is a product of exactly \(k'\) reflections, and \(\det R=(-1)^{k'}\) forces \(k'\) to be even. Pad with \((r-k')/2\) pairs \(H_uH_u=I\). A single reflection has determinant \(-1\), so \(W_0H_u\), a rank-one update in the \(O(n)\)-orbit, is not in the image.

Dimension. A generic element is determined by its moved space \(\mathrm{im}(R-I)\in\mathrm{Gr}(r,n)\), of dimension \(r(n-r)\), together with a fixed-point-free element of \(SO(r)\) on it, an open set of dimension \(r(r-1)/2\). The sum is \(rn-r(r+1)/2\), and the generic fibre has dimension \(rn-d\). The identity \(H_aH_b=H_{H_ab}H_a\) shows that the Hurwitz move \((u_i,u_{i+1})\mapsto(H_{u_i}u_{i+1},u_i)\) preserves \(\rho\).

(b) Normalise \(\hat w=w/\|w\|\). A direct computation gives

\[\tfrac{d}{d\varepsilon}\big(H_{w+\varepsilon a}H_{w+\varepsilon b}\big)\big|_{\varepsilon=0}=\tfrac{2}{\|w\|}\big(v\hat w^\top-\hat wv^\top\big),\qquad v=(I-\hat w\hat w^\top)(a-b).\]

Every pair equals \(I\) at \(q_0\), so the differential of the product is the sum of the pair differentials, and \(S_1=W_0\cdot\{\sum_iv_i\wedge\hat w_i\}\) with \(v\wedge w:=vw^\top-wv^\top\). This set lies in \(\{\Omega:P_{U^\perp}\Omega P_{U^\perp}=0\}\), because \(y^\top(v\hat w^\top-\hat wv^\top)x=0\) for \(x,y\perp\hat w\). Conversely, that space is spanned by the \(e_a\wedge e_b\) with \(e_a\in U\), and each is a combination of the elements \(\hat w_i\wedge e_b\). Its dimension is \(\dim\mathfrak{so}(n)-\dim\mathfrak{so}(n-k)=kn-k(k+1)/2\), and \(W_0\) is injective. Finally \(d-\dim S_1=k(2n-3k-1)/2\gt0\) for \(n\ge2k+2\).

(c) The Cayley map \(C(\Omega)=(I+\Omega)(I-\Omega)^{-1}\) is a diffeomorphism from a neighbourhood of \(0\in\mathfrak{so}(n)\) onto a neighbourhood of \(I\in SO(n)\), and \(C(\Omega)-I=2\Omega(I-\Omega)^{-1}\), so \(\operatorname{rank}(C(\Omega)-I)=\operatorname{rank}\Omega\). Composing with the linear embedding \(R\mapsto W_0R\) identifies the germs. \(\Sigma_r\) is a closed cone, and it is not linear, because a rank-\(r\) and a rank-\(2\) skew matrix can sum to rank \(r+2\le n\). A closed cone that is \(C^1\) at its vertex is linear, so \(0\) is singular. The apex statement uses (b); a singular image alone would not give a positive deficit.

(d) Over a generic point of the meet, the LoRA fibre is a \(GL_r\)-orbit and the \(SO(n)\) fibre is a point, since \(W_0\) is injective; this gives \(d+r^2\). Over \(W_0\) itself the fibre product contains \(\{(B,0,I)\}\), of dimension \(mr\), so it is not pure-dimensional. If \(C(\Omega)x=-x\), then \(y=(I-\Omega)^{-1}x\) satisfies \((I+\Omega)y=-(I-\Omega)y\), so \(y=0\). For density, perturb the angles \(\pi\) inside the same moved planes. For the Cayley–Neumann factor \(R\), a point \(W_0R\) of \(W_0\cdot O(n)\) needs \(R\) orthogonal, since \(W_0\) is injective, that is \((I-Q^4)^2=I\), which for small \(Q\) forces \(Q=0\). Conversely a skew \(Q\) of rank \(2\) inside one block gives \(\operatorname{rank}(R-I)=2\) with \(R\) not orthogonal, a point of LoRA \(\cap\) OFT outside \(\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{HRA}_r\). ∎

Numerical check: at \(n=9\) the paired-init Jacobian has rank \(8\) against generic rank \(15\) for \(r=2\), and \(15\) against \(26\) for \(r=4\).

More: symmetries, Gram–Schmidt and odd rank

The evident continuous symmetries of HRA's gauge are the \(r\) scalings \(u_i\mapsto c_iu_i\); the braid group also acts, by Hurwitz moves. HRA with Gram–Schmidt (apply_GS=True) has the smaller image \(W_0(I-2P_U)\), of dimension \(r(n-r)\), which never contains \(W_0\). For odd \(r\), HF falls back to a random initialisation, and \(\det R=(-1)^r\) shows that no odd-\(r\) HRA can start at \(W_0\).

CreditThe sharp Cartan–Dieudonné theorem, that a rotation moving a \(k\)-dimensional space is a product of exactly \(k\) reflections, is due to Scherk (1950); see Artin's Geometric Algebra. Householder parametrisations in deep learning go back to Mhammedi et al., 2016, and Lie-algebra charts such as the Cayley map to Lezcano-Casado and Martínez-Rubio, 2019. Yuan et al., 2024 proposed HRA as a bridge between low-rank and orthogonal adaptation, which the theorem makes precise. The paired initialisation is read from the HF PEFT HRA layer.

HRA's “bridge between low-rank and orthogonal adaptation” is, at the level of images, the meet of LoRA with the rotation orbit. It strictly contains LoRA \(\cap\) OFT for exactly orthogonal OFT and is incomparable with it for HF's default OFT. Compare Proposition I.8(a), where LoRA-XS (Bałazy et al., 2024) is a genuine categorical product \(\mathrm{FA}\times\mathrm{FB}\). That HRA's default initialisation is an apex is new here. Its first-order collapse is the multiplicative twin of LoRA's dead \(A\)-direction (Lemma II.4), and whether it slows early training is open (Exposé XI). Rebasing commutes with the meet, so after \(K\) cycles re-HRA reaches \((W_0+\mathcal M_{\le Kr})\cap W_0\cdot SO(n)\) (Theorem V.3, corollary 5).

In Figure 3, choose HRA and press “Reset to \(q_0\)”. The first-order strip reads \(\dim S_1=3\) against \(d=5\), the values of (a) and (b) at \(n=4\), \(r=2\). Move \(u_2\) and the Gram, spectrum and rank lamps stay lit together; “\(u=(e_1,e_2)\)” lands on \(W_0H_{e_1}H_{e_2}\), which exact Cayley OFT never reaches.

LOFT fixes one leaf of the meet

The proof of Theorem II.7 already shows how the meet is built. It is the union of the orbits \(W_0\cdot SO(V)\) over the \(r\)-dimensional input subspaces \(V\), one subgroup orbit for each point of the Grassmannian \(\mathrm{Gr}(r,n)\). Call each orbit a leaf. Leaves overlap along lower-rank strata, so this is a union and not a partition, but a generic point lies on exactly one, fixed by its moved space \(\mathrm{im}(R-I)\). HRA's reflection vectors move the leaf as it trains. LOFT (Zhao et al., 2026) freezes the leaf and makes the choice of leaf the design variable, which the paper calls the adaptation support.

2026One component of LoCO (Nguyen et al., 2026) is the Cayley rotation of a low-rank skew generator \(UV^\top-VU^\top\), with \(U,V\in\mathbb R^{n\times r}\). For injective \(W_0\) and \(2r\le n-2\), its image is \(\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{HRA}_{2r}\) minus the points \(W_0R\) at which \(R\) has eigenvalue \(-1\), so like HRA it moves its leaf while it trains. Writing the generator as \(ZJZ^\top\) with \(Z=[U\ V]\) shows that its generic fibre is an orbit of the linear group \(Sp(2r,\mathbb R)\), acting by \(Z\mapsto ZM\). The non-linear gauge of Theorem II.7(a) therefore comes from the reflections, not from the meet. Its sum of several components is in general only approximately orthogonal, and then leaves the meet.
Example (LOFT: a leaf, and how to choose it). Let \(P_r\in\mathbb R^{r\times n}\) have orthonormal rows spanning \(V\), and let \(O(V)=\{I+P_r^\top(T-I)P_r:T\in O(r)\}\) be the orthogonal maps that fix \(V^\perp\) pointwise. LOFT trains \(\rho(E)=W_0\big(I+P_r^\top(Q(E)-I)P_r\big)\) with \(E\in\mathfrak{so}(r)\), \(E_0=0\), so \(|M|=r(r-1)/2\). We read it with an exactly orthogonal \(Q\) (the exponential, or the Cayley map \(C\)); the released code defaults to a five-term Cayley–Neumann series with \(R^\top R=(I-X^6)^2\), which is only approximately orthogonal, so like HF's default OFT (Corollary II.6) that version leaves its leaf.
  1. One leaf. If \(W_0P_r^\top\) has rank \(r\), then \(\big(W_0+W_0P_r^\top\,\mathbb R^{r\times r}P_r\big)\cap W_0\cdot O(n)=W_0\cdot O(V)\). For the principal support \(P_r=V_r^\top\) the first set is the image of LoRA-XS (this needs \(\sigma_r(W_0)\gt0\)), so the leaf is LoRA-XS \(\cap\,W_0\cdot O(n)\). Principal-support LOFT reaches an open dense part of its component \(W_0\cdot SO(V)\); for injective \(W_0\) and a Cayley \(Q\), its image is exactly LoRA-XS \(\cap\) the image of exact full-block Cayley OFT.
  2. Leaves over the Grassmannian. If \(W_0\) is injective, \((W_0+\mathcal M_{\le r})\cap W_0\cdot SO(n)=\bigcup_{V\in\mathrm{Gr}(r,n)}W_0\cdot SO(V)\), which for even \(r\) is \(\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{HRA}_r\) (Theorem II.7(a)). LOFT's image is the whole leaf \(W_0\cdot SO(V)\) with the exponential, and an open dense part of it with a Cayley map, which never reaches eigenvalue \(-1\).
  3. A regular base point. If \(W_0P_r^\top\) has rank \(r\), then \(S_1=W_0P_r^\top\mathfrak{so}(r)P_r\) has dimension \(r(r-1)/2=d(M)\): LOFT starts at a smooth point of its leaf and has no gauge, while HRA's paired initialisation is an apex (Theorem II.7(b)). For \(r\ge4\) there is no smooth pointed arrow LOFT\(\to\)HRA at that initialisation, although the images are nested (test O3).
  4. Choosing the leaf. If \(Q'(0)=\mathrm{id}\) (the exponential, or \(E\mapsto C(E/2)\)), the gradient pulled back to \(E\) at \(\theta_0\) is \(P_r\operatorname{skew}(W_0^\top G)P_r^\top\) with \(G=\nabla L(W_0)\); the Cayley map \(C\) itself doubles it, which does not change the best support. Its squared norm is at most \(2\sum_{k\le r/2}\mu_k^2\), where \(\pm i\mu_k\) are the eigenvalues of \(\operatorname{skew}(W_0^\top G)\), with equality when \(V\) spans the invariant subspace of the top \(\lfloor r/2\rfloor\) pairs.
(expand)

(a) A rotation \(T\in O(r)\) gives \(W_0(I+P_r^\top(T-I)P_r)=W_0+W_0P_r^\top(T-I)P_r\), so “\(\supseteq\)” holds. Conversely let \(W=W_0+BXP_r\) with \(B=W_0P_r^\top\), and suppose \(W=W_0S\) with \(S\in O(n)\). Then \(WW^\top=W_0W_0^\top\), and expanding with \(P_rP_r^\top=I_r\) gives \(B\,(X+X^\top+XX^\top)\,B^\top=0\). Since \(B\) has full column rank, \(X+X^\top+XX^\top=0\), that is \((I+X)(I+X)^\top=I\), so \(T=I+X\in O(r)\). For \(P_r=V_r^\top\) we have \(B=U_r\Sigma_r\), and \(\{U_r\Sigma_rXV_r^\top\}=\{U_rR\,\Sigma_rV_r^\top\}\) through \(R=\Sigma_rX\Sigma_r^{-1}\), which is LoRA-XS's image. For injective \(W_0\), \(W_0S\) determines \(S\), so meeting with exact full-block Cayley OFT keeps exactly the \(S(T)\) with \(T\in SO(r)\) and no eigenvalue \(-1\), which is the Cayley image.

(b) Every \(S\in SO(V)\) has \(\operatorname{rank}(S-I)\le r\), which gives “\(\supseteq\)”. Conversely, if \(W_0S-W_0\) has rank at most \(r\) and \(W_0\) is injective, then \(\operatorname{rank}(S-I)\le r\). The fixed space \(F=\ker(S-I)\) has dimension at least \(n-r\), and \(F^\perp\) is invariant. Any \(r\)-dimensional \(V\supseteq F^\perp\) works: \(S\) is the identity on \(V^\perp\subseteq F\) and has determinant \(1\) on \(V\).

(c) \(D\rho_0(E)=W_0P_r^\top Q'(0)(E)P_r\), with \(Q'(0)\) invertible. If this vanishes, then \(W_0P_r^\top Q'(0)(E)=0\) because \(P_r\) has full row rank, and \(E=0\) because \(W_0P_r^\top\) has full column rank. So \(\dim S_1=\lvert M\rvert\ge d(M)\ge\dim S_1\). A smooth pointed arrow would give \(S_1(\mathrm{LOFT})\subseteq S_1(\mathrm{HRA})\) (Observation I.6), and by Theorem II.7(b) and injectivity this asks every \(\Omega\in\mathfrak{so}(V)\) to satisfy \(P_{U^\perp}\Omega P_{U^\perp}=0\). Since \(\dim(V\cap U^\perp)\ge r-r/2\ge2\), any two orthonormal \(a,b\in V\cap U^\perp\) give \(a\wedge b\) violating it.

(d) With \(Q'(0)=\mathrm{id}\), \(\tfrac{d}{dt}L\big(\rho(tE)\big)\big|_{0}=\langle G,W_0P_r^\top EP_r\rangle=\langle P_rW_0^\top GP_r^\top,E\rangle=\langle P_r\operatorname{skew}(W_0^\top G)P_r^\top,E\rangle\), since \(E\) is skew. The bound is a Ky Fan inequality for the skew matrix \(\operatorname{skew}(W_0^\top G)\) compressed to \(V\). ∎

Numerical check (\(n=8\), \(m=10\), \(r=2,4,6\)): the leaf identity, the union, the arrows into exact Cayley OFT and LoRA-XS, and \(\dim S_1=r(r-1)/2\) all hold to about \(10^{-15}\).

CreditLOFT, the SkewGrad and GradSVD supports, part (d) and its bound (their Proposition 2), the reading of each support as selecting a Lie subgroup (their Remark 1) and the suggestion of dynamic supports (their Remark 3) are from Zhao et al., 2026. Their Table 1 writes full and block OFT, GOFT, BOFT, HRA and PSOFT as choices of support, width, depth and transform in a multi-factor version of the same primitive. Parts (a)–(c) place the single-factor method inside Theorem II.7. PSOFT is Wu et al. (ICLR 2026); LoRA-XS is Bałazy et al., 2024.

Orthogonal methods differ in which leaves they use and how they combine them. Block OFT, GOFT and BOFT multiply rotations of fixed coordinate subspaces; HRA moves its leaf while it trains; PSOFT takes the principal leaf and adds two tori; LOFT takes one leaf, chosen before training from \(W_0\) or from the gradient at \(\theta_0\). By part (d), SkewGrad is the orthogonal counterpart of the alignment mechanism of Proposition II.3, and GradSVD transplants the gradient-based frames of LoRA-GA. With the leaf frozen, merge-and-restart gains nothing. With finitely many supports revisited across restarts, the reach is the orbit of the connected group whose Lie algebra the \(\mathfrak{so}(V_k)\) generate (Theorem V.3(b)); numerically, generic supports with \(r\ge2\) generate all of \(SO(n)\) once \(\lceil n/r\rceil\) of them are used. The principal support does not move when it is re-selected after a merge, and data-chosen supports have to be checked case by case.

DoRA is a torus saturation of LoRA

DoRA normalises each row of LoRA's matrix \(V=W_0+BA\) and multiplies row \(i\) by a learned magnitude \(\mu_i\). That is one row scaling, by \(\mu_i/\|v_i\|\), so every DoRA weight is a diagonal matrix times a LoRA weight, and conversely up to a repair for vanishing rows. We write \(\mathrm{Diag}_m\) for the monoid of diagonal matrices, \(\mathrm{Mon}_m=T_m\rtimes S_m\) for the monomial matrices, and \(N\) for row normalisation.

Proposition II.8 (DoRA is LoRA followed by an output scale, at the level of images). Let \(1\le r\le\min(m,n)\) and \(s\ne0\), and let DoRA's magnitude \(\mu\in\mathbb R^m\) be per output neuron, as in HF PEFT. Let \(W_0\) have no zero row, and let \(\mathcal U\) be the open set of matrices with no zero row. Then \[\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{DoRA}_r=\{\mathrm{diag}(\mu)\,N(V):V\in(W_0+\mathcal M_{\le r})\cap\mathcal U,\ \mu\in\mathbb R^m\}=\mathrm{Diag}_m\cdot(W_0+\mathcal M_{\le r}),\] which is exactly the image of “\(\mathrm{LoRA}_r\) followed by a trainable output scale \(\ell\in\mathbb R^m\)”. With \(\mu\) restricted to \((\mathbb R^\times)^m\), or equivalently after intersecting with \(\mathcal U\), \(\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{DoRA}_r\cap\mathcal U=T_m\cdot(W_0+\mathcal M_{\le r})\cap\mathcal U\). Moreover:
  • \(d(\mathrm{DoRA}_r)\le\min\big(mn,\ r(m+n-r)+m\big)\). At a parameter point with every \(\mu_i\ne0\) and \(X=V-W_0\in\mathcal M_r\), with \(U=\mathrm{col}\,X\) and \(R=\mathrm{row}\,X\), the Jacobian rank is \(r(m+n-r)+m-\dim\ker\big(\delta\mapsto P_{U^\perp}\mathrm{diag}(\delta)W_0P_{R^\perp}\big)\); where some \(\mu_i=0\) it is smaller when \(r\ge2\), and for \(r=1\) when some row \(w_{0,i}\) with \(\mu_i=0\) lies outside \(R\). For generic \(W_0\), \(d=\min\big(mn,\ r(m+n-r)+m\big)\) Num, which equals \(r(m+n-r)+m\) iff \((m-r)(n-r)\ge m\), as for all practical shapes. For special \(W_0\) it is smaller, for instance \(r(m+n-r)+m-r\) for a rank-one \(W_0\) without zero rows when \(r\lt m\) and \(r\le n-2\) Num.
  • The image covariance group (Definition II.14) contains \(\mathrm{Mon}_m\times GL_n\).
  • At \(B=0\), in HF's magnitude coordinates, \(\Delta W=\big(\mathrm{diag}(\mu_i/\|w_{0,i}\|)-I\big)W_0\), which can have rank up to \(\operatorname{rank}W_0\), so DoRA escapes LoRA's rank bound.
(expand)

\(\mathrm{diag}(\mu)N(V)=\mathrm{diag}(\mu_i/\|v_i\|)\,V\in\mathrm{Diag}_m\cdot V\). Conversely, take \(\mathrm{diag}(\ell)V\) with \(V=W_0+X\), \(\operatorname{rank}X\le r\), and let \(Z\) be the set of zero rows of \(V\). Put \(V'=W_0+(I-\varepsilon\sum_{i\in Z}E_{ii})X\). Its rows outside \(Z\) are those of \(V\), its rows in \(Z\) are \(\varepsilon w_{0,i}\ne0\), and its update still has rank \(\le r\). With \(\ell'_i=0\) on \(Z\) and \(\ell'_i=\ell_i\) elsewhere, \(\mathrm{diag}(\ell')V'=\mathrm{diag}(\ell)V\) and \(V'\in\mathcal U\). For the \(T_m\) statement, \(DV\in\mathcal U\) with \(D\) invertible forces \(V\in\mathcal U\).

Dimension. \(\rho_{\rm DoRA}=\rho_{\rm LoRA+scale}\circ\varphi\) with \(\varphi(\mu,B,A)=(\mu_i/\|v_i\|,B,A)\), a diffeomorphism of DoRA's domain, so ranks may be computed for \((D,X)\mapsto D(W_0+X)\). For invertible \(D\) the differential at \((D,X)\) is \(D\) times the one at \((I,X)\) after \(\delta\mapsto D^{-1}\delta\). Its image at \((I,X)\) is \(\{\mathrm{diag}(\delta)V\}+T_X\mathcal M_r\); the first summand has dimension \(m\) since \(V\in\mathcal U\), and \(\mathrm{diag}(\delta)V\in T_X\mathcal M_r\) iff \(P_{U^\perp}\mathrm{diag}(\delta)VP_{R^\perp}=0\), where \(VP_{R^\perp}=W_0P_{R^\perp}\). A zero \(\mu_i\) kills row \(i\) of the LoRA directions, leaving only \(\delta_iv_i\) there, where an invertible magnitude also gives every \(e_iy^\top\) with \(y\in R\). Since \(v_i-w_{0,i}\in R\), the rank falls below the formula by at least \(\dim\mathrm{span}(w_{0,i},R)-1\) for each such \(i\), which is positive when \(r\ge2\) or \(w_{0,i}\notin R\). For the generic value, a proof sketch: with bases \(E\) of \(U^\perp\) and \(F\) of \(R^\perp\) the kernel map is \(\delta\mapsto\sum_i\delta_i(E^\top e_i)\otimes(F^\top W_0^\top e_i)\), and \(m\) generic points of the Segre cone in \(\mathbb R^{m-r}\otimes\mathbb R^{n-r}\) span \(\min(m,(m-r)(n-r))\) dimensions.

Covariance. A monomial \(g\) normalises \(\mathrm{Diag}_m\), and \(g\mathcal M_{\le r}h^{-1}=\mathcal M_{\le r}\), so \(g\,\mathrm{Diag}_m(W_0+\mathcal M_{\le r})h^{-1}=\mathrm{Diag}_m(gW_0h^{-1}+\mathcal M_{\le r})\). ∎

Numerical check: Jacobian rank \(9=mn\) at \((m,n,r)=(3,3,2)\), where \(r(m+n-r)+m\) gives \(11\); \(36\) at \((8,8,2)\), matching; \(24\) for a rank-one \(W_0\) at \((6,6,2)\). With one \(\mu_i=0\) at \((8,8,2)\) the rank drops to \(34\).

CreditDoRA is due to Liu et al., 2024. Its magnitude–direction split follows weight normalisation (Salimans and Kingma, 2016). From a different direction, Zhang et al., 2025 argue that the magnitude of the update is the main driver of LoRA's performance.
More: which weight, which norm

Images refer to the merged weight; in training mode with LoRA dropout, HF's DoRA forward is not of the form \(Wx\). The DoRA paper's notation normalises “column-wise”, while both its official code and HF PEFT normalise each output row.

At the level of images, DoRA's “magnitude–direction decoupling” is LoRA plus \(m\) free output gains; everything else happens in training. Under gradient flow the exact gradient of the normalised parametrisation is a change of metric, while the detached backward of the DoRA paper (Sec. 4.3) and HF PEFT (weight_norm.detach()) is a pseudo-gradient that no metric describes. Under Adam the coordinates matter too, as \(\mu_i\) starts at \(\|w_{0,i}\|\) and \(\ell_i\) at \(1\). Prediction X.2 (Conjecture D) uses the equal images to set up a controlled comparison.

The covariance group \(\mathrm{Mon}_m\times GL_n\) decides where Observation II.15 applies. One orthogonal rotation of the residual stream of a gain-fused pre-norm model, as in SliceGPT, acts on the input side of \(q\), \(k\), \(v\), gate and up, where DoRA is \(GL_n\)-covariant, so DoRA on those boxes reaches the same functions after the rotation as before; on \(o\) and down the sufficient condition fails. Training outcomes are a separate question.

The torus ladder

Write a weight as \(W_0\odot(J+Z)\), with \(J\) the all-ones matrix. Output scalings use \(Z=(\ell-\mathbf 1)\mathbf 1^\top\), of rank one; two-sided scalings use \(ab^\top-J\), of rank at most two; HiRA allows any \(Z=BA\) of rank at most \(r\). These choices form a ladder of images, and no rung can fill a zero of \(W_0\).

Proposition II.9 (the torus ladder).
  1. HiRA. \(\rho(B,A)=W_0\odot(J+BA)\); on linear and convolutional layers HF PEFT has \(B_0=0\) and no scale factor. For every \(W_0\), \(\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{HiRA}_r=W_0+D_{W_0}(\mathcal M_{\le r})\) with \(D_{W_0}:X\mapsto W_0\odot X\). If \(W_0\) has no zero entries, \(D_{W_0}\in GL(\Theta)\), so in the slice at \(\theta_0\) HiRA is LoRA transported by a diagonal automorphism, with the same gauge and the same \(d=r(m+n-r)\) for \(r\le\min(m,n)\). Always \(\operatorname{rank}\Delta W\le r\cdot\operatorname{rank}W_0\). The transport depends on \(\theta_0\) (Proposition II.13).
  2. Scalings. Output scalings \(\mathrm{diag}(\ell)W_0=W_0\odot(J+(\ell-\mathbf 1)\mathbf 1^\top)\) and input scalings \(W_0\mathrm{diag}(\ell)=W_0\odot(J+\mathbf 1(\ell-\mathbf 1)^\top)\) have images in \(\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{HiRA}_1\). Output-side examples are (IA)³ on keys and values, SSF's post-linear scale and DoRA's magnitude. Input-side examples are (IA)³'s feed-forward vector, which scales the input of \(W_{\rm down}\), and a LayerNorm gain folded into the next linear map. The fold is exact when the norm's output feeds only linear maps, and it then ties the gain across them (Q, K and V, or gate and up). Two-sided scalings \(\mathrm{diag}(a)W_0\mathrm{diag}(b)=W_0\odot(ab^\top)\) lie in \(\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{HiRA}_2\).
  3. Orbit dimension. The two-sided torus orbit \(\{\mathrm{diag}(a)W_0\mathrm{diag}(b)\}\) has dimension \(m+n-c(W_0)\), where \(c(W_0)\) is the number of connected components of the bipartite support graph of \(W_0\), an isolated vertex counting as its own component.
  4. RoAd. \(\mathrm{RoAd}_1\) (the paper's main variant, HF road_1) is \(W=RW_0\) with \(R\) block-diagonal in scaled rotations \(\alpha_i\mathrm{Rot}(\theta_i)\) of output pairs, the left action of \(CO^+(2)^{m/2}\cong(\mathbb C^\times)^{m/2}\), or (IA)³ over \(\mathbb C\). It multiplies the input Gram matrix of each output pair by \(\alpha_i^2\), and since \(\alpha_i\) may be \(0\) its image is the orbit closure, a real-linear space of dimension \(m\) when no output-pair block of \(W_0\) is zero. \(\mathrm{RoAd}_2\) and \(\mathrm{RoAd}_4\) let each block range over the monoid \(M_2(\mathbb R)\), with group part \(GL_2(\mathbb R)\); their linear image \(\{\mathrm{blockdiag}(R_i)W_0\}\) has dimension \(2m\) when every pair block has rank \(2\), the Gram statement and “over \(\mathbb C\)” fail, and \(\mathrm{RoAd}_4\) has a positive-dimensional gauge.
  5. Zeros and covariance. Every \(W\) reachable by HiRA, (IA)³ or SSF vanishes wherever \(W_0\) does, \(Z(W_0)\subseteq Z(W)\), and this persists under merging. New zeros can appear (\(\ell_i=0\), or \((J+BA)_{ij}=0\)), so the zero pattern itself is not invariant. The image covariance group of output-scaled boxes ((IA)³ on \(k,v\); post-linear SSF) is \(\mathrm{Mon}_m\times GL_n\), and that of input-scaled boxes ((IA)³ on \(W_{\rm down}\)) is \(GL_m\times\mathrm{Mon}_n\). HiRA's is exactly \(\mathrm{Mon}_m\times\mathrm{Mon}_n\).
(expand)

(a) \(W_0\odot(J+BA)=W_0+W_0\odot BA\). \(D_{W_0}\) is diagonal in the standard basis of \(\Theta\), and invertible iff no entry of \(W_0\) vanishes; pushforward along a linear isomorphism preserves dimension and gauge. For the rank, \(W_0\odot(BA)=\sum_k\mathrm{diag}(b_k)W_0\mathrm{diag}(a_k)\), summed over the \(r\) columns \(b_k\) of \(B\) and rows \(a_k\) of \(A\), which has rank \(\le r\operatorname{rank}W_0\).

(b) Direct computation, using \(ab^\top-J=[a,-\mathbf 1][b,\mathbf 1]^\top\in\mathcal M_{\le2}\).

(c) The tangent map at \((\mathbf 1,\mathbf 1)\) is \((\alpha,\beta)\mapsto\big((\alpha_i+\beta_j)(W_0)_{ij}\big)\). Its kernel requires \(\alpha_i+\beta_j=0\) along every support edge, so it is one-dimensional on each connected component. Torus orbits are homogeneous, so the dimension is the same at every point of the orbit.

(d) A \(2\times n\) real block \(X\) is a complex row in \(\mathbb C^n\), and \(\alpha\mathrm{Rot}(\theta)\) acts on it as multiplication by \(\alpha e^{i\theta}\). So the image for one pair is \(\mathbb C\cdot X\), of real dimension \(2\) iff \(X\ne0\), and \((\alpha\mathrm{Rot}X)^\top(\alpha\mathrm{Rot}X)=\alpha^2X^\top X\). In \(\mathrm{RoAd}_2\) each row of a block is an arbitrary vector of \(\mathbb R^2\) written in polar form.

(e) Hadamard multiplication and row or column scaling preserve the zeros of \(W_0\).

(IA)³ and SSF. A monomial \(g\) normalises the diagonal monoid, so \(\mathrm{Mon}_m\times GL_n\) lies in the covariance group of output scalings. Conversely, take \(W_0=e_jc^\top\). Then \(\mathrm{Im}\,M(gW_0h^{-1})=\mathrm{span}\{e_i:i\in\mathrm{supp}(ge_j)\}\,c^\top h^{-1}\), while \(g\,\mathrm{Im}\,M(W_0)\,h^{-1}=\mathbb R\,(ge_j)c^\top h^{-1}\). Equality forces \(\lvert\mathrm{supp}(ge_j)\rvert=1\) for every \(j\), so \(g\) is monomial. This works for all \(m,n\). Input-side scalings are the transpose.

HiRA. Monomial pairs commute with Hadamard products up to relabelling, so \(\mathrm{Mon}_m\times\mathrm{Mon}_n\) lies in the covariance group. Conversely, take \(W_0=E_{ij}\). Then \(\mathrm{Im}=\mathbb R\,E_{ij}\), so covariance under \((g,h)\) forces \(\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{HiRA}_r(uv^\top)=\mathbb R\,uv^\top\) with \(u=ge_i\) and \(v=h^{-\top}e_j\). But that image contains \(uv^\top+(u\odot p)(v\odot q)^\top\) for all \(p,q\), which leaves the line \(\mathbb R\,uv^\top\) unless \(\lvert\mathrm{supp}\,u\rvert=\lvert\mathrm{supp}\,v\rvert=1\). So every \(ge_i\) and every \(h^{-\top}e_j\) has a single nonzero entry, and \(g\) and \(h\) are monomial. ∎

Numerical check: \(\mathrm{RoAd}_1\) orbit dimension \(8\) and \(\mathrm{RoAd}_2\) dimension \(16\) at \(m=8\).

More: pointings, LayerNorm folds and HF PEFT details

As a morphism, \(h(\ell)=(\ell-\mathbf 1,\mathbf 1^\top)\) lands in \(\mathrm{HiRA}_1\) pointed at \((0,\mathbf 1^\top)\), not at HiRA's random-\(A\) default. A LayerNorm gain folds exactly for the input norms of pre-norm blocks and a final norm before an untied head, but not for post-sublayer norms (Gemma-2 and 3) or QK-norms; LayerNorm biases, like SSF's shift, leave \(W\) alone. HF HiRA on embeddings starts at \(A_0=0\). HF RoAd pairs output \(j\) with \(j+g/2\) in groups of size \(g\) and also rotates the bias; nothing above depends on the pairing.

Credit(IA)³ is due to Liu et al., 2022, SSF to Lian et al., 2022, HiRA to Huang et al., 2025, RoAd to Liao and Monz, 2024, and LayerNorm tuning to Zhao et al., 2023. Feature-wise scaling as a building block goes back at least to FiLM (Perez et al., 2017).

(IA)³, SSF's scales, folded LayerNorm gains and HiRA are rungs \(W_0\odot(J+Z)\). DoRA and RoAd can fill zeros of \(W_0\); they become rungs only when \(W_0\) has no zero entries, where every image germ is a rung and the ladder by itself constrains nothing. HiRA's “high rank” is a property of a frame. Its transport \(D_{W_0}\) depends on \(\theta_0\) and does not preserve rank. Two re-based cycles reach \(W_0\odot(J+Z_1)\odot(J+Z_2)\), whose relative update \(Z_1+Z_2+Z_1\odot Z_2\) has rank up to \(\min(2r+r^2,m,n)\), against \(2r\) for LoRA (Proposition II.13).

With HF defaults, (IA)³ scales the output side of the attention projections it targets (\(k\) and \(v\), or \(q\) and \(v\)) and the input side of down, so the residual rotation above, acting on the other side, leaves its reachable functions unchanged. HiRA's covariance group stops at \(\mathrm{Mon}_m\times\mathrm{Mon}_n\) and fails the sufficient condition of Observation II.15. That alone does not prove its reachable functions change; Conjecture A says they do, and Prediction X.4 is the test.

ReviewCorollary II.6, Theorem II.7 and Propositions II.8 and II.9 were repaired after two rounds of adversarial review; the referee record lists what changed.

The groups of this exposé see orbits. LoRA's cone is governed instead by the rank of the update, read off a cut in a tensor network, while the size of an image is a dimension, which behaves like a volume. Rank and volume can move in opposite directions, the subject of Exposé II.C.

Exposé II.C

Rank is a cut, dimension is a volume

At a fixed budget, the narrowest cut of a method’s diagram bounds the rank it can reach, the dimension of what it can reach is a separate count, and the two can move in opposite directions.

Rank lives on cuts and dimension lives in volumes. Count the wrong one and you will compare the wrong things.

a working maxim
In one minute
  • A method’s update rank is bounded by the narrowest cut of its tensor-network diagram. The dimension \(d(M)\) of what it can reach is its parameter count minus the directions that change nothing.
  • The two can move in opposite directions. At the budget of LoRA\(_8\) on a \(4096\times4096\) layer, LoHa reaches rank 16 and at least 8,159 fewer dimensions; GraLoRA reaches rank 16 at LoRA’s dimension, with every block capped at rank 4.
  • Kronecker sums are LoRA across another cut of the same four-leg tensor, and HiRA is LoRA after \(X\mapsto W_0\odot X\) (for \(W_0\) without zero entries). Such changes of frame keep dimension and move rank.
  • LoRA respects every change of a layer’s coordinates and so reaches the same set of functions after a function-preserving rotation of the network; PiSSA, MiLoRA and CorDA splits respect only rotations and uniform scalings.

PEFT papers usually report a method’s expressiveness as a rank and its cost as a parameter count. A third number, the dimension of the set of weights the method can reach, usually goes unreported.

Draw a method as a network of boxes and wires. The rank of its update is limited by the narrowest place where the network can be cut in two, so rank is a cut invariant. The dimension counts the independent directions in which the method can move the weights; we call it a volume invariant, loosely, since no measure is computed. In Figure 4, several methods raise one while lowering the other.

Rank is a cut

In LoRA every signal crosses the inner wire of dimension \(r\) between \(A\) and \(B\), so \(\Delta W=BA\) has rank at most \(r\). The argument works for any method drawn as a tensor network, a string diagram in \((\mathbf{FinVect},\otimes)\). Split the boxes into an input side and an output side, and the network factors through the wires that cross.

CreditString diagrams go back to Joyal and Street (1991), and the copy spider to the Frobenius algebra of a basis (Coecke, Pavlovic and Vicary). Cut bounds on rank are standard tensor-network reasoning (Cichocki et al., 2016; Kolda and Bader, 2009). The PEFT reading is ours.
Theorem II.10 (cut-rank bound).

Let \(\Gamma\) be a tensor network, evaluated in the symmetric monoidal category \((\mathbf{FinVect},\otimes)\), with input legs \(V\) and output legs \(U\). Every wire and open leg is assumed incident to a vertex: insert an identity box on any bare strand, or equivalently count each bare input–output wire as crossing every cut. A crossing hyperedge (copy spider) contributes its dimension once. Partition the vertices into \(\mathcal I\sqcup\mathcal O\). The weight \(w\) of the cut is the product of the dimensions of the internal wires between the two sides and of the open legs attached on the wrong side (input legs at \(\mathcal O\), output legs at \(\mathcal I\)). Then

\[\operatorname{rank}[\![\Gamma]\!]\le\min_{\rm cuts}w,\]

and rank is subadditive over formal sums. The bound refers to a chosen presentation of the network.

(expand)

Write \(V_{\mathcal I},V_{\mathcal O}\) for the input legs attached to \(\mathcal I\) and \(\mathcal O\), \(U_{\mathcal I},U_{\mathcal O}\) likewise for the outputs, and \(C\) for the tensor product of the cut wires. Contracting each side gives \(T_{\mathcal I}:V_{\mathcal I}\to U_{\mathcal I}\otimes C\) and \(T_{\mathcal O}:C\otimes V_{\mathcal O}\to U_{\mathcal O}\). Then

\[[\![\Gamma]\!]=(\mathrm{id}_{U_{\mathcal I}}\otimes T_{\mathcal O})\circ(T_{\mathcal I}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{V_{\mathcal O}})\]

factors through \(U_{\mathcal I}\otimes C\otimes V_{\mathcal O}\), whose dimension is \(w\). Symmetric monoidal structure suffices; no cups or caps are needed. Subadditivity is \(\operatorname{rank}(X+Y)\le\operatorname{rank}X+\operatorname{rank}Y\).

Numerical check: no violation in 283 random networks.

∎

Without the convention on bare strands, \(I_d\otimes BA\) would violate the bound. The bound also depends on the drawing. A naive expansion of HRA’s product of reflections has \(2^r-1\) terms, while a telescoping sum gives the sharp bound \(r\).

More: the bound for common methods
methodpresentation usedbound on \(\operatorname{rank}\Delta W\)

HRAtelescoping sum \(R-I=\sum_k(H_k-I)H_{k+1}\cdots H_r\)\(r\)
Givens circuit with \(g\) gatesthe same telescoping sum\(2g\)

GraLoRAsum of \(k^2\) block LoRAs\(kr\)

The “high-rank PEFT” literature consists of networks whose min-cut avoids an \(r\)-dimensional bottleneck. A large min-cut is necessary for high rank and does not guarantee it, and attaining it can cost volume.

Dimension is a volume

Moving \(q\in Q\) along a fibre of \(\rho\) changes nothing. For LoRA, \((Bg^{-1},gA)\) gives the same weight as \((B,A)\) for every \(g\in GL_r\), so \(r^2\) of the \(r(m+n)\) parameters are spent on a symmetry and the image has dimension \(r(m+n-r)\). This is the expressive dimension \(d(M)\) of Definition I.5; \(\lvert M\rvert-d(M)\) is the gauge waste.

CreditThe quotient \((\mathbb R^{m\times r}_*\times\mathbb R^{r\times n}_*)/GL_r\) is the classical geometry of fixed-rank matrices (Mishra et al., 2012). Putterman et al., 2024 build learners on LoRA weights that respect this \(GL_r\) symmetry.
More: what “gauge” means here

“Gauge” means the symmetry group of a parametrisation. There is no connection, and away from the full-rank stratum the fibres jump in dimension.

Proposition II.12 (gauge census: rank is a cut, dimension is a volume).

(a) \(d(M)\le\lvert M\rvert-\dim(\text{generic gauge orbit})\), with equality when the gauge acts locally transitively on generic fibres.

(b) Values for one \(m\times n\) box (\(r\le\min(m,n)\) except for VeRA). The exhibited gauge is the generic gauge of Definition I.5 exactly when (a) is an equality, which can fail for LoHa and DoRA once the cap \(mn\) binds.

methodbudget \(\lvert M\rvert\)exhibited gauge (dim)\(d(M)\)max rank
LoRA\(_r\)\(r(m+n)\)\(GL_r\) \((r^2)\)\(r(m+n-r)\)\(r\)
GraLoRA (\(k\times k\) blocks, rank \(r/k\) each; HF default hybrid_r=0)\(r(m+n)\)\(\prod GL_{r/k}\) \((r^2)\)\(r(m+n-r)\)\(\min(kr,m,n)\)
LoHa\(_{r_1,r_2}\)\((r_1+r_2)(m+n)\)\(GL_{r_1}\times GL_{r_2}\times{}\)diagonal torus \((r_1^2+r_2^2+m+n-1)\)\(\min(mn,F)\) [Num]; \(F\), below, is a proved upper bound\(\min(r_1r_2,m,n)\)
HRA\(_r\) (\(\operatorname{rank}W_0=n\), apply_GS=False)\(rn\)non-linear; fibre dimension \(r(r+1)/2\); exhibited Lie gauge \((\mathbb R^\times)^r\)\(rn-r(r+1)/2\)\(r\)
DoRA\(_r\)\(r(m+n)+m\)\(GL_r\)\(\min\big(mn,\) \(r(m+n-r)+m\big)\) for generic \(W_0\) [Num]up to full
VeRA\(m+r\)\(\mathbb R^\times\)\(m+r-1\) [Num]\(\min(r,m,n)\)
KronA \(A\otimes B\)\(m_1n_1+m_2n_2\)\(\mathbb R^\times\)\(m_1n_1+m_2n_2-1\)\(\min(m_1,n_1)\min(m_2,n_2)\)
TT / Tucker\(\sum\) cores\(\prod_eGL_{r_e}\)\(\lvert M\rvert-\sum_er_e^2\) at minimal ranksup to full
linear methods (LoRA-FA, LoRA-XS, MiSS, FourierFT, C3A, masks)\(\lvert M\rvert\)translations by the kernelrank of the linear map; \(=\lvert M\rvert\) when injectivevaries

In the LoHa row,

\[F=r_1(m+n-r_1)+r_2(m+n-r_2)-(m+n-1).\]

FourierFT is injective unless its sampled frequencies contain a conjugate pair \((u,v),(-u,-v)\), and then \(d=\lvert M\rvert-\#\{\text{pairs}\}\). At \(768\times768\) with \(1000\) frequencies a pair occurs in about half of the draws.

(c) Equal-budget trades. LoHa\(_{r,r}\) and LoRA\(_{2r}\) both cost \(2r(m+n)\). If \(2r\le\min(m,n)\) and \(r^2\le\min(m,n)\), LoHa reaches at least \((m+n-1)-2r^2\) fewer dimensions, exactly that many when \(F\) is attained. If also \(m+n\ge2r^2+2\), so that this gap is positive, then for \(r\ge3\) LoHa gains rank up to \(r^2\gt2r\) in exchange, and for \(r\le2\) (where \(r^2\le2r\)) \(\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{LoHa}_{r,r}\subsetneq\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{LoRA}_{2r}\). GraLoRA\(_k\) has the budget and dimension of LoRA\(_r\) and maximum rank \(\min(kr,m,n)\), but caps every block at rank \(r/k\). A generic rank-\(r\) update has rank \(r\) on every block, so for \(k\ge2\) and \(kr\le\min(m,n)\) the images of LoRA\(_r\) and GraLoRA\(_k\) are incomparable.

(expand)

(a) The generic fibre contains the generic gauge orbit, and \(d=\lvert M\rvert-\dim(\text{generic fibre})\).

LoHa. The torus acts by

\[(B_1,A_1,B_2,A_2)\mapsto(D_1B_1,\,A_1D_2,\,D_1^{-1}B_2,\,A_2D_2^{-1}),\]

which preserves the product because \((D_1XD_2)\odot(D_1^{-1}YD_2^{-1})=X\odot Y\). The joint action of \(GL_{r_1}\times GL_{r_2}\times T_m\times T_n\) has generic stabiliser \(\{(cI,c^{-1}I,cI,c^{-1}I)\}\), so the orbit has dimension \(r_1^2+r_2^2+m+n-1\). Sketch for \(r_1\lt\min(m,n)\): an infinitesimal stabiliser \((\xi_1,\xi_2,\delta_1,\delta_2)\) satisfies \(\delta_1B_1=B_1\xi_1\), so the column space of \(B_1\) is invariant under the diagonal \(\delta_1\). A generic subspace of dimension below \(m\) is invariant only under scalar diagonals, so \(\delta_1=\xi_1=cI\). The equation for \(A_1\) then forces \(\delta_2=-cI\), and those for \(B_2,A_2\) give \(\xi_2=-cI\). The remaining corner shapes were checked numerically up to \(7\times7\). Trivially \(d\le mn\).

GraLoRA. The image is a product of \(k^2\) cones \(\mathcal M_{\le r/k}(\tfrac mk\times\tfrac nk)\), of total dimension \(k^2\cdot\tfrac rk\cdot\tfrac{m+n-r}{k}=r(m+n-r)\). For the rank, write block \((i,j)\) as \(U_{ij}V_{ij}^\top\) with \(U_{ij}\in\mathbb R^{m/k\times r/k}\) and \(V_{ij}\in\mathbb R^{n/k\times r/k}\). Then \(\Delta W=\mathrm{blockdiag}(L_1,\dots,L_k)\,S\), with \(L_i=[U_{i1}\cdots U_{ik}]\in\mathbb R^{m/k\times r}\) and \(S\in\mathbb R^{kr\times n}\) stacking the \(V_{ij}^\top\), each supported on column block \(j\). For generic blocks and \(kr\le\min(m,n)\), each \(L_i\) has full column rank \(r\) and \(S\) has full row rank \(kr\), so \(\operatorname{rank}\Delta W=kr\). In general the rank is at most \(\min(kr,m,n)\). Placing rank-\(r/k\) blocks only on the diagonal would give rank \(r\), not \(kr\).

(c). Since \(d(\mathrm{LoHa}_{r,r})\le F\) and \(d(\mathrm{LoRA}_{2r})=2r(m+n-2r)\) for \(2r\le\min(m,n)\),

\[d(\mathrm{LoRA}_{2r})-d(\mathrm{LoHa}_{r,r})\ge2r(m+n-2r)-F=(m+n-1)-2r^2.\]

LoHa\(_{r,r}\) maps into LoRA\(_{r^2}\) by the face-splitting product \(h=(B_1\bullet B_2,(A_1^\top\bullet A_2^\top)^\top)\), one of the arrow rules of the atlas. For \(r\le2\) this gives \(\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{LoHa}_{r,r}\subseteq\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{LoRA}_{r^2}\subseteq\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{LoRA}_{2r}\), and a positive gap makes the inclusion strict. Without \(m+n\ge2r^2+2\) the argument fails: at \((m,n,r)=(4,4,2)\) both images are 16-dimensional, and a numerical solver found exact LoHa\(_{2,2}\) decompositions of every target tried.

Other rows. The KronA image is a Segre cone, and \(\operatorname{rank}(A\otimes B)=\operatorname{rk}A\operatorname{rk}B\). HRA is Theorem II.7 for \(r\le n-2\); the cases \(r\in\{n-1,n\}\) were checked numerically. DoRA is Proposition II.8. The TT and Tucker counts are the cited results, which assume minimal ranks. The remaining rows are standard. For FourierFT, \(\mathrm{Re}\circ\mathrm{ifft2}\) sends the frequencies \(p\) and \(-p\) to the same real pattern.

Numerical check: LoHa dimensions \(38,59,93,101\) at \((m,n,r)=(8,7,2),(12,10,2),(12,10,3),(20,16,2)\), and \(16=mn\) at \((4,4,2)\), where the uncapped formula gives \(17\). GraLoRA dimensions \(48,64,108\) at \((m,n,r,k)=(8,8,4,2),(12,8,4,2),(12,12,6,3)\); generic rank \(8=kr\) at \((16,16,4,2)\) and \(12=\min(m,n)\) at \((12,12,6,3)\).

∎

When \(m+n\le2r^2+1\), still with \(2r\le\min(m,n)\) but without needing \(r^2\le\min(m,n)\), the count gives no gap, and wherever \(F\) is attained, as the numerical checks find, LoHa\(_{r,r}\) matches or exceeds LoRA\(_{2r}\) in dimension as well as rank, for example at \(r=64\) on a \(4096\times4096\) layer and at LyCORIS-style \(r=32\) on Stable Diffusion layers 320 to 640 wide, where \(r^2\gt\min(m,n)\).

CreditLoHa is the low-rank Hadamard product of FedPara (Hyeon-Woo et al., 2021); GraLoRA is Jung et al., 2025. The Tucker gauge and dimension are due to Koch and Lubich (2010) and Uschmajew and Vandereycken (2013), the TT case to Holtz, Rohwedder and Schneider (2012), building on Oseledets, 2011.

When \(2r^2\) is small next to \(m+n\), as on LLM projections at the usual ranks, LoHa reaches high rank on a thin, curved subvariety, because its Hadamard product spends a whole torus on gauge. GraLoRA keeps LoRA’s dimension and caps every block’s rank.

CreditThe intrinsic-dimension experiments of Li et al., 2018 and Aghajanyan et al., 2020 measure how many random directions a task needs. \(d(M)\) counts the directions a method’s image has, a complementary notion.

Kronecker adapters cut the other way

If \(m=m_1m_2\) and \(n=n_1n_2\), an \(m\times n\) matrix is a tensor with output legs \(U_1,U_2\) and input legs \(V_1,V_2\). LoRA’s rank is the rank across \(\{U_1U_2\}\mid\{V_1V_2\}\); a sum of \(k\) Kronecker products has rank at most \(k\) across \(\{U_1V_1\}\mid\{U_2V_2\}\). The cuts disagree sharply, since \(I\otimes I\) is one Kronecker term of full rank.

Theorem II.11 (Kronecker incomparability).

Write \(\mathbb R^m=\mathbb R^{m_1}\otimes\mathbb R^{m_2}\) and \(\mathbb R^n=\mathbb R^{n_1}\otimes\mathbb R^{n_2}\), and view \(\Delta\in\mathbb R^{m\times n}\) as a 4-leg tensor. Let \(\operatorname{rk}\) be the rank across \(\{U_1U_2\}\mid\{V_1V_2\}\) and \(\operatorname{rk}_\otimes\) the rank across \(\{U_1V_1\}\mid\{U_2V_2\}\). Put \(\kappa_1:=\min(m_1,m_2)\min(n_1,n_2)\) and \(\nu:=\min(m_1,n_1)\min(m_2,n_2)\).

(a) \(\operatorname{rk}_\otimes\Delta\le k\) iff \(\Delta=\sum_{i\le k}C_i\otimes D_i\). Every \(\Delta\) has \(\operatorname{rk}_\otimes\Delta\le\min(m_1n_1,m_2n_2)\).

(b) \(\operatorname{rk}(C\otimes D)=\operatorname{rk}C\cdot\operatorname{rk}D\), while \(\operatorname{rk}_\otimes(C\otimes D)=1\) for \(C,D\ne0\). So \(\nu\) is the largest rank of a single Kronecker product.

(c) If \(u\in\mathbb R^m\) and \(v\in\mathbb R^n\) have Schmidt ranks \(\rho_u,\rho_v\), then \(\operatorname{rk}_\otimes(uv^\top)=\rho_u\rho_v\). A generic rank-one update therefore has Kronecker rank \(\kappa_1\). For aligned splits, \((m_1-m_2)(n_1-n_2)\ge0\), which include equal square splits and HF PEFT’s default factorisation, \(\kappa_1=\min(m_1n_1,m_2n_2)\) is the largest Kronecker rank.

(d) Incomparability. For \(1\le r\lt\nu\) and \(1\le k\lt\kappa_1\), the sets \(\mathcal M_{\le r}\) and \(\mathcal K_{\le k}:=\{\Delta:\operatorname{rk}_\otimes\Delta\le k\}\) of unconstrained \(k\)-term Kronecker sums are incomparable: a generic rank-one update lies in the first and not the second, and a Kronecker product of full-rank factors lies in the second and not the first. For \(m=n=N\) and \(m_1=m_2=n_1=n_2=s\), \(\kappa_1=\nu=s^2=N\), and \(I_N\) is such a product.

(e) Constrained Kronecker methods. Let \(\mathrm{Im}\,M\subseteq W_0+\mathcal K_{\le k}\), and give LoRA\(_r\) its zero-initialised image \(W_0+\mathcal M_{\le r}\). Then \(\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{LoRA}_r\not\subseteq\mathrm{Im}\,M\) whenever \(k\lt\kappa_1\). For LoKr, \(\Delta W=C\otimes BA\) with \(C\in\mathbb R^{m_1\times n_1}\) and inner rank \(r'\le\min(m_2,n_2)\), the maximum rank \(\min(m_1,n_1)\cdot r'\) is attained, so \(\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{LoKr}_{r'}\subseteq\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{LoRA}_r\) iff \(r\ge\min(m_1,n_1)\,r'\), and otherwise the two images are incomparable, provided \(\kappa_1\gt1\).

(expand)

(a) and (b) follow from the Van Loan–Pitsianis rearrangement \(\mathcal R(C\otimes D)=\mathrm{vec}\,C\,\mathrm{vec}\,D^\top\), which is wire-bending; \(\mathcal R\Delta\) is an \((m_1n_1)\times(m_2n_2)\) matrix, and \(\operatorname{rk}_\otimes\Delta=\operatorname{rank}\mathcal R\Delta\).

(c) Write the Schmidt decompositions \(u=\sum\sigma_ix_i\otimes y_i\) and \(v=\sum\tau_jz_j\otimes w_j\). Then

\[uv^\top=\sum_{i,j}\sigma_i\tau_j\,(x_iz_j^\top)\otimes(y_iw_j^\top)\]

is an operator-Schmidt decomposition with Frobenius-orthonormal families and \(\rho_u\rho_v\) nonzero coefficients. Generic \(u,v\) have full Schmidt ranks \(\min(m_1,m_2)\) and \(\min(n_1,n_2)\). If \(m_1\le m_2\) and \(n_1\le n_2\), then \(\kappa_1=m_1n_1\le m_2n_2\), and symmetrically in the other aligned case.

(d) follows from (b) and (c). (e) A generic \(W_0+uv^\top\) lies in \(\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{LoRA}_r\) and, by (c), outside \(W_0+\mathcal K_{\le k}\) when \(k\lt\kappa_1\). For LoKr, (b) gives \(\operatorname{rank}(C\otimes BA)=\operatorname{rk}C\cdot\operatorname{rk}(BA)\le\min(m_1,n_1)\,r'\), with equality for full-rank \(C\) and \(BA\) of rank \(r'\). So LoKr reaches rank above \(r\) exactly when \(r\lt\min(m_1,n_1)\,r'\); and LoKr lies in \(W_0+\mathcal K_{\le1}\), so the first clause applies when \(\kappa_1\gt1\).

Numerical check: Schmidt ranks \((1,3)\to3\), \((2,3)\to6\), \((3,3)\to9\), each with ordinary rank \(1\); for the \(2\times4\) split of \(\mathbb R^8\), \(\operatorname{rk}_\otimes(uv^\top)=4\), the largest Kronecker rank there; LoKr with \(s=4\), \(r'=1\) never exceeds rank \(4\).

∎
CreditThe rearrangement \(\mathcal R\) and the nearest-Kronecker-product problem are due to Van Loan and Pitsianis, 1993. Kronecker-sum layers entered deep learning with PHM (Zhang et al., 2021) and Compacter; KronA (Edalati et al., 2022) and LoKr (Yeh et al., 2023) brought them to weight updates.

Unconstrained Kronecker sums are LoRA across a different cut, so calling them higher-rank LoRAs mixes two cuts; with fewer than \(\kappa_1\) terms they miss a generic rank-one update. LoKr with inner rank \(r'\) lies inside LoRA\(_r\) only once \(r\ge\min(m_1,n_1)\,r'\), which is 192 on 768-wide layers and 512 on 4096-wide layers at HF’s default \(r'=8\). Below that, the images are incomparable.

More: LoKr in HF PEFT, Compacter and MPO

HF PEFT factors LoKr’s second matrix as \(BA\) only when its r is below half the larger of \(m_2,n_2\). Otherwise the second factor is a full matrix, LoKr is an unconstrained Kronecker product, and its maximum rank is \(\nu\). On a \(768\times768\) layer split \(24\otimes32\), the maximum rank jumps from 360 at r=15 to 768 at r=16. The decompose_both option also caps \(C\) at rank r.

Compacter’s PHM weights are Kronecker-structured weights inside a nonlinear bottleneck adapter, and MPO adapters are a multi-core reparametrisation that is lossless at full bond dimension. The image comparison does not transfer to either without more work.

Pred Under a loss whose only minimiser is the target weight, LoRA\(_1\) fits \(W_0+uv^\top\) (generic \(u,v\)) exactly and no Kronecker sum with fewer than \(\kappa_1\) terms does. HF PEFT’s factorisation is aligned, so \(\kappa_1\) is maximal there (576 for \(768=24\times32\)). At equal budget neither family should dominate across tasks.

In Figure 5, at rest, a generic \(uv^\top\) on the split \(4\otimes4\) has rank 1 and Kronecker rank 16. LoRA\(_1\) fits it with 32 parameters and a Kronecker sum with 16 terms and 512. For \(I_{16}\) the costs trade places.

Transport of structure

An invertible linear change of frame fixing \(\theta_0\) carries dimension, gauge, first-order image and degree along, since they are defined from \(\rho\) and its differential. It carries rank along only when it respects rows and columns, which the Kronecker rearrangement, Fourier and wavelet frames and the Hadamard rescaling by \(W_0\) do not.

Proposition II.13 (transport of structure).

For \(T\in GL(\Theta)\) put \(\tau_T(\theta)=\theta_0+T(\theta-\theta_0)\) and \(T_*M:=(Q,q_0,\tau_T\circ\rho)\).

  • \(T_*\) is a strict automorphism of \(\mathsf{Meth}(\Theta,\theta_0)\), with inverse \((T^{-1})_*\). It maps \(S_1\) to \(T\,S_1\) and preserves \(d\), \(g\), the gauge group, the first-order deficit and polynomial degree. It does not preserve rank. Extended to natural families by \((T_*M)(\theta):=(Q,q_0,\theta+T(\rho_\theta-\theta))\) for a fixed \(T\), it does not preserve the covariance group.
  • \(K^{T_*M}=T\,K^M\,T^\top\) for every \(T\). An update rule that sees the loss only through \(q\mapsto L(\rho(q))\), with its state on \(Q\), runs \(T_*M\) on \(L\) exactly as it runs \(M\) on \(L\circ\tau_T\). Rules that read \(\nabla_\theta L\) or act on \(\theta\) (LoRA-Pro-style projections, weight decay or clipping in \(\theta\)) need \(T\) orthogonal, which is exactly when \(\tau_T\) is a Frobenius isometry.
  • For an additive family with fixed shape \(C\) and a fixed \(T\), transport commutes with rebasing: \(R_K(T_*M)=\tau_T(R_K(M))\) (Thm V.3(a), with shape \(TC\)). This can fail when \(T\) depends on \(\theta_0\), and for action-type families even when \(T\) is fixed.

Instances. A sum of \(k\) Kronecker products is \(\mathcal R^{-1}_*\mathrm{LoRA}_k\), with \(Q\) reshaped by vec, dynamics included for coordinatewise optimizers; KronA is \(\mathcal R^{-1}_*\mathrm{LoRA}_1\), and LoKr is the same with its second factor constrained to \(\mathrm{vec}\,\mathcal M_{\le r'}\). Started at \(\theta_0\), FourierFT (with no conjugate frequency pair sampled), C3A and WaveFT (when its padded inverse transform is invertible) are coordinate masks in a real cosine, circulant or wavelet frame, and LoRA-XS, SVFT and TinyLoRA are masks in the SVD frame of \(W_0\). HiRA is \((D_{W_0})_*\mathrm{LoRA}\), \(D_{W_0}:X\mapsto W_0\odot X\), when \(W_0\) has no zero entries; since \(D_{W_0}\) depends on \(\theta_0\), two rebasing cycles reach relative rank up to \(\min(2r+r^2,m,n)\), against \(2r\) for transported ReLoRA.

(expand)

\(D(\tau_T\circ\rho)=T\,D\rho\). This gives the statements about \(S_1\), the ranks of the differential, \(d\), \(g\) and the deficit, and \(K^{T_*M}=TD\rho\,\Lambda\,D\rho^\top T^\top\). Since \(T_*\) does not touch \(Q\), \(L\circ\rho_{T_*M}=(L\circ\tau_T)\circ\rho_M\) exactly, so the two objectives on \(Q\) coincide, and the \(\theta\)-trajectories are conjugate by \(\tau_T\). A rule that projects \(\nabla_\theta L\) by a Frobenius least-squares step moves by \(-(D\rho^\top T^\top TD\rho)^+D\rho^\top T^\top\nabla L\) on \(T_*M\), which matches its step on \(M\) for \(L\circ\tau_T\) when \(T^\top T=I\). Orthogonal \(T\) also keep the Frobenius spectrum of \(K\) and isometry-invariant losses such as \(\|\theta-\theta^\star\|^2\). Coordinate permutations of \(Q\) commute with every coordinatewise update rule.

Rebasing: for an additive family with shape \(C\), the transported family has shape \(TC\) at every base, and \(R_K\) is a \(K\)-fold Minkowski sum, so \(R_K(T_*M)=\theta_0+T(C+\dots+C)=\tau_T(R_K(M))\) by induction. For OFT with \(T=2I\), \(n=2\) and a unit base vector, two transported cycles reach norm \(9\), while the transported closure has norms in \([1,3]\).

The circulant identity is the DFT diagonalisation, and \(\mathrm{Re}\circ\mathrm{ifft2}\) sends conjugate frequencies to the same real pattern. For HiRA, two cycles reach \(W_0\odot(J+Z_1)\odot(J+Z_2)\), and \((J+Z_1)\odot(J+Z_2)=J+Z_1+Z_2+Z_1\odot Z_2\).

Numerical check: \(\|K^{T_*M}-TK^MT^\top\|=2\times10^{-14}\) for a Gaussian \(T\); Adam on \(T_*M\) with \(L\) and on \(M\) with \(L\circ\tau_T\) agree to \(8\times10^{-11}\); two HiRA cycles give relative-update rank \(3,8,15\) at \(r=1,2,3\) (\(m=n=30\)).

∎
More: the instances in detail
  • The mask readings assume a start at \(\theta_0\). HF’s defaults for FourierFT (a random spectrum unless init_weights=True) and C3A (xavier_uniform) start away from it.
  • FourierFT: HF’s \(\Delta W=s\cdot\mathrm{Re}(\mathrm{ifft2}(S))\), with a fixed scaling \(s\), is sparse in the real cosine half of the real Fourier frame. Each sampled conjugate pair \((u,v),(-u,-v)\) adds one dimension of gauge. The image lies in the point-symmetric subspace \(\Delta W_{j,l}=\Delta W_{-j,-l}\), and the frame is orthogonal only up to scale (self-conjugate frequencies have twice the squared norm).
  • C3A: block-circulant. Over \(\mathbb R\) the frame is given by the powers \(P^k\) of the cyclic shift (Gram matrix \(bI\)). Over \(\mathbb C\), \(\mathrm{circ}(w)=F^{-1}\mathrm{diag}(Fw)F\) for first column \(w\), and the real form has \(2\times2\) rotation–scaling blocks. HF adds a factor \(1/\mathrm{in\_features}\).
  • SHiRA, FISH and Trainable Tokens are masks in the standard frame.
  • MiSS/Bone in its default (balance) mode is \(\Delta W=D(\mathbf 1_g^\top\otimes I_r)\), LoRA-FA with a frozen summing projection. Its BAT mode, blockwise \(\Delta W=\mathrm{blocks}(W_0)\,D+D\), is linear in \(D\) and depends on \(\theta_0\).
  • LoKr: in HF PEFT the rank constraint on the second factor is active only below the threshold given after Theorem II.11, and decompose_both also constrains the first factor.
CreditThe transported methods are FourierFT (Gao et al., 2024), WaveFT (Bilican et al., 2025), C3A (Chen et al., 2024), LoRA-XS (Bałazy et al., 2024), SVFT (Lingam et al., 2024), TinyLoRA (Morris et al., 2026), SHiRA (Bhardwaj et al., 2024), FISH (Sung et al., 2021), MiSS (Kang and Yin, 2024) and HiRA (Huang et al., 2025). Si et al., 2024 organise PEFT around the SVD frame of \(W_0\), a related unification.

Rank is invariant under \(GL_m\times GL_n\) and changes under \(GL(\Theta)\), so “high rank” claims (MoRA, HiRA, LoKr, C3A, SHiRA, WaveFT) depend on a frame that the network function does not respect. Transport keeps every isomorphism invariant (\(d\), \(g\), gauge group, deficit, apex or smooth type, degree) and conjugates the dynamics. In Figure 4 the HiRA dot sits at LoRA’s height and at full rank (Prop II.9(a)).

Pred At matched learning-rate sweeps, the gap between a transported method and its source (Kronecker sums against LoRA, Fourier or wavelet methods against masks) tracks how well each image aligns with a full fine-tuning \(\Delta W\), more than it tracks optimizer effects.

The plug, and a reporting rule
